## 3. Geopolitics — The World in Our Era

### Cormoria (our setting) `[LOCKED]`

- **Meritocratic empire.** Commoners can rise through military, business, or academia to become Viziers. Has nobility and royalty but isn't *defined* by them.
- **Wealth and stability** — the richest, most powerful nation of our era.
- **Court structure:** Queen-Regent + four Viziers + nine noble Trial Houses + military hierarchy capped by the rank of Tarasque.
- **Cultural values:** meritocracy, altruism, Pokémon-philosophy over Pokémon-as-weaponry.
- **Currently vulnerable** — the cults have spent years infiltrating its institutions.

### Paldea (Eden's homeland) `[LOCKED]`

- **Recently founded empire in consolidation phase** — Eden's "kingdom" is the imperial core.
- **Rigid two-tier caste society** — peasant or royal/noble, no middle. Caste is birth-determined.
- **Currently the country with the closest formal relationship to Cormoria**, but the relationship is asymmetric — Paldea is poor, divided, and resents Cormorian prosperity.
- **Serfs cannot attend school or learn to read** unless they are born into merchant or noble class.
- **The current king is a pivotal figure** — a "greedy ignorant nepotic trust-fund baby pawn" who wishes to please his fellow nobles. His later mistake with the Treasures Of Ruin is the *catastrophic event* that forces Paldea's slow reform across the next 1,500 years.
- **Recruitment ground for the Ruin Cults**, especially their Paldean merchant-class and minor-noble sympathizers.

### The Western Coalition: Kanto, Johto, Hoenn `[LOCKED]`

- **Three despotic isolationist nations**, each dominated by a single major noble family (think Tokugawa-era Japan).
- **Behind Cormoria in technology** but xenophobic and ambitious.
- **They instigated violence first** — broke early trade agreements with Cormoria, started border conflicts.
- **Kanto seized the Sevii Islands** from a Cormorian settlement there. This is one of the war's specific casus belli.
- **They unite against Cormoria** out of envy of Cormorian wealth and fear of Cormorian military strength.
- **The Kanto-Johto war** that Osrid fought in was Cormoria-defensive — defending against this coalition's aggression.

### The Long Arc — How the Modern Pokémon World Forms `[LOCKED]`

This is the *meta-design thesis* of the game's relationship to canon:

Across the next ~1,500–2,000 years:
- Cormorian meritocratic values **culturally bleed into Kanto, Johto, and Hoenn**
- The despotic dynasties are eventually replaced by the **early Pokémon League system**
- **The League's structural origin is Cormoria's Trials + Champion model** (with later influence from Kalos and Hisui)
- The modern, peaceful, trainer-friendly Pokémon world we know is the *outcome* of Cormorian values winning the long cultural war — even though Cormoria itself does not survive as a state

**Osrid loses his name, his memory, his nation. Cormoria loses everything. But the *idea* of Cormoria wins, two millennia later, in the institution of the Pokémon League itself.**

This is not spelled out in dialogue. It is the *capstone subtext* of the game, intelligible to lore-attentive players who think about what the modern Pokémon world's origins must have been.

### Kalos's role `[OPEN]`

- AZ's Ultimate Weapon War happened ~1,000 years before our game
- Kalos is in long post-war recovery during our era
- Cormoria and Kalos may have a relationship as fellow non-Western powers, but this is undeveloped
- `[OPEN]` Does Kalos play any direct role in our game's plot?

## 4. The Cormorian Empire (Internal Structure)

### Political structure `[LOCKED]`

- **Imperial court structure.** The Empire is ruled by a monarch with a tiered support structure of Viziers and Trial Houses.
- **Currently ruled by Queen-Regent Rhydia** — the protagonist's younger sister.
- **Court hierarchy:** Queen-Regent → 4 Viziers (one of whom is Umbra) → 9 noble Trial Houses → military officer corps → commoners.
- `[OPEN]` Who is the absent monarch Rhydia is regent for? Where is Cormoria's geographic capital?

### Military culture `[LOCKED]`

- Empire maintains a formal military with **rank-specific ceremonial masks** for decorated officers
- **Rank hierarchy** (the full structure is documented in **Section 2 — Cormorian military rank hierarchy**):
  - **Cadet → Lieutenant → Strategos → Windshear → Rainhawk → Tarasque → Dawnstar → Vizier**
  - First three ranks (Cadet, Lieutenant, Strategos) constitute ~90% of military personnel
  - **Rainhawk** is the watershed rank — empire promotes to Rainhawk only those they believe could eventually lead a Trial, head a guild, or be knighted into nobility
- The protagonist earned his **Rainhawk** mask during the Kanto-Johto-Hoenn war. Mask is a distinctive Noh-styled ceremonial design with an eye-of-Horus pattern on the front, awarded to him after he was severely scarred during a Sevii Islands operation (see Section 5).
- Nemo Korolev rose through every tier from Cadet to **Dawnstar** by merit alone, faster than any officer in Cormorian history — including Eldred. He then voluntarily stepped down to the 9th Trial Baron seat (see Section 7 — Nemo profile).
- **The Champion is usually a former Vizier, a Tarasque or Dawnstar, or a particularly prodigal royal.** New Champions emerge once in a few generations.

### The 9 Trials + 4 Viziers + Champion system `[LOCKED]`

This is the empire's institutional structure for recognizing martial-Pokémon excellence, and the **proto-template for the modern Pokémon League system**.

**The 9 Trials** are presided over by **Trial Barons** — typically the heads of noble houses (see Section 2 for the noble house structure). Each Trial Baron specializes in a single monotype — the type identity is hereditary, passed down across generations within the noble house, *except* for Nemo Korolev (the 9th Trial Baron) who is the only non-noble in the group.

**Currently named Trial Barons (and Baronesses) `[UPDATED v0.9.7]`:**
- **1st Trial: Kimaris Langerin (Ghost)** — former Champion of Cormoria. Family are keepers of the Mirroh Necropolis (which decays into "Mirroh Base Camp Graveyard" in DM's era). Family naming convention: occult-traditional names. Ace: **Crowley** — a unique shiny Spiritomb with **Prankster** ability, brought into the material realm after thousands of failed summoning attempts. Moves: Curse, Strength Sap, Shadow Claw, Protect. Trial level cap: 23.
- **2nd Trial: Reid Ashland (Fire)** — prodigy of House Ashland. Age 23, head of household-by-default since Sable's disappearance. Lives in the Ashland Labyrinth inside Mt. Ceram. Ace: **unique shiny Heatran** with **Earth Eater** ability (Ground immunity replaces canonical Flash Fire). Moves: Torch Song, Magnet Bomb, Freeze-Dry, + holds Passho Berry. Trial level cap: 30. Note: Reid retires from Trial Baron at ~70% through the story when Sable returns and resumes household leadership; Reid retires to study under Vizier Umbra.
- **3rd Trial: Manus Surge (Electric)** — head of House Surge. Promoted to Trial Baron in the 10-year gap during Osrid's overseas service. Father of Boran Surge (Osrid's overseas combat mentor; see Section 7). Far more emotional in temperament than his son. Has been openly aware of Boran's cold "Pokemon as weapons" philosophy and has tried unsuccessfully to counsel him toward more compassionate ideas. Trial level cap: 35-40. **Thematically loaded fight:** Osrid arrives at this Trial confronting the *paternal source* of his combat mentor's corruption — not the direct corruptor, but the man who shaped the corruptor. Manus's emotional grief over what his son became may surface in pre/post-Trial dialogue. Ace and full team `[OPEN]`.
- **8th Trial: Brie Moray (Fairy) `[LOCKED v0.9.7]`** — Silas Moray's 15-year-old niece, the Domdaniel-chosen heir of House Moray. **Specializes in Fairy rather than the Moray family's traditional Psychic** as a moral rebellion against her uncle's perceived corruption. The empire granted her the Trial Baroness seat through special dispensation when her Fairy-mastery proved exceptional — see Section 7 — Brie Moray for the full character entry. Ace: **Domdaniel** (shiny Scream Tail, Fairy/Psychic, Huge Power — House Moray's generational Mythical-tier ace, the Pokemon that rejected Silas in childhood and chose Brie). Trial level cap: 50-55 (penultimate Trial). **Thematically loaded fight:** the player faces House Moray's generational ace *not* in the hands of its formal head (Silas) but in the hands of the niece Domdaniel actually chose. *The cosmic-tier rejection of Silas is embodied in the encounter the player wins.*
- **9th Trial: Nemo Korolev (Ground)** — Sinnohian war-orphan turned Cormorian Dawnstar turned Trial Baron. The 9th Trial Baron seat was vacated by the destruction of House Jalviss. Nemo's identity as **"the Usurper"** (killer of Harbinger Alaric, holder of Ting-Lu) is revealed during the Trial battle's second phase (see Section 7). Ace 1: **Manticore** — unique shiny Gliscor with **Adaptability** ability. Moves: Beak Blast, Earthquake, Sleep Talk, Rest. Ace 2 (Phase 2): **Ting-Lu** itself. Trial level cap: `[OPEN — appropriate to the 9th position, presumably 60+]`.
- **Trial Barons 4-7: `[OPEN]`** — four remaining Trial Baron seats to be designed. We have type-tradition houses queued for some of these seats but the Trial Baron identities (specific names, aces, levels) are not yet locked:
  - **One seat (Grass-type) is held by House Alban.** Alban Trial Baron identity `[OPEN]`. **Thematically loaded for Osrid:** if the player completes the Alban Trial, they are facing the very house that knowingly or unknowingly sheltered Osrid's "Nomad Alban" cover identity for 10 years. Whether the Alban Trial Baron knew "Nomad" was Osrid is `[OPEN]`.
  - **One seat (Flying-type) is held by House Raptora.** Raptora Trial Baron identity `[OPEN]`.
  - **One seat (Dark-type) is held by House Nightfall.** Nightfall Trial Baron identity `[OPEN]`. **Thematically loaded for Osrid:** he is half-Nightfall by blood and has never met the current Trial Baron of his late father's house.
  - **One seat (Normal-type) `[LOCKED v0.9.7]`** — the final remaining Trial Baron slot, type-locked but everything else `[OPEN]` (house, name, ace, level, narrative role).

The four `[OPEN]`-numbered slots cover Grass (Alban), Flying (Raptora), Dark (Nightfall), and Normal — exact Trial-number assignments TBD.

**The 4 Viziers `[UPDATED v0.9.7]`** serve as attendants to the Champion and to the Royal Family. They are the empire's most senior officials in their respective domains:
- **Vizier of Education — Cadmus Umbra** (Water-type master; Pokémon Philosopher; proto-Professor figure)
- **Vizier of War — Glaive** (Dragon-type master; the second Solomonar in 1700+ years; ascends to the post mid-game after his revelation event)
- **Vizier of Commerce — Silas Moray** (Psychic-type master; head of House Moray; secret 2IC of the Sword Cult)
- **Vizier of `[OPEN portfolio]`** `[LOCKED v0.9.7]` — the **female Rock-type master** Vizier (name and house `[OPEN]`). Her boyhood-Tyrunt-revival anecdote is locked (Section 7 — Madame Roma; her ace is a shiny Tyrantrum, regal and well-known across Cormoria as the result of Roma's fossil revival when she was a girl). Her specific Vizier portfolio (Industry? Infrastructure? Justice? Religious affairs separate from Templar Arceus?) is `[OPEN]`. See Section 7 — Female Rock-master Vizier for character profile.

**The Champion** is the highest authority in the realm — currently Rhydia (Section 5 / 7). Defeating the Viziers is the prerequisite for challenging the Champion.

**The full meritocratic gate sequence:**
1. **9 Trials** (presided by Trial Barons)
2. **The Paragon Gauntlet** (introduced in Section 11 Principle 3) — six recurring rivals fight in succession, no breaks/healing between fights, only one healing item allowed
3. **4 Viziers**
4. **Champion (Rhydia)**

This becomes, across two millennia, the 8 Gyms → Elite Four → Champion structure of the modern Pokémon League.

### Templar Arceus Order `[LOCKED]`

A religious-civil order present in every Cormorian city. Templars serve Arceus directly and maintain civic infrastructure on Arceus's behalf. Key functions:

- **Operate the Tavern network** (see below)
- **Provide the Braviary inter-city travel service** (Section 9)
- **Maintain religious-civil order** as Arceus's intermediaries in the empire
- **Operate as informal intelligence-gatherers** (their cross-city network is excellent for noticing political and cult activity)

**Internal division:** The Templar Arceus order is **internally divided.** While the majority of Templars are doctrinally and personally opposed to the Ruin Cults (which they view as heretical anti-Arceus), **some Templars are secretly cult-aligned** — typically Silas-Moray-influenced or Baradus-bought. This creates an ongoing tension: the order *should* be a natural enemy of the cults, but its corruption limits its effectiveness.

**Templar Pokemon partnerships:** Templars typically carry **Braviary** (Normal/Flying, the *honest civic service* form). Silas Moray, in contrast, carries a **shiny Hisuian Braviary named Thauma** (Psychic/Flying — the manipulator's form). The fact that Silas's national bird is the *aberrant* form is itself a worldbuilding statement: the corrupted Templar institution has produced its own corrupted patron.

**Leadership — the Archbishop `[LOCKED v0.9.7]`:** The Templar Arceus order is led by **Archbishop Ambrose Caymen**, a Steel-type master and one of the project's most thematically complex characters. He sits at the top of the order's *warrior-priest* tradition (the militant side of Arceus's service, alongside the civic-administration side). His childhood friendship with Nemo Korolev and his cult-pressured indecision through the early-to-mid game culminate in a player fight that resolves his moral paralysis through partnership-demonstration. See Section 7 — Ambrose Caymen for the full character entry.

`[OPEN]` Specific Templar leadership structure, individual Templar named characters, the political-religious tension between Templars and Cormorian state authority.

### Taverns — Empire-era Pokémon Centers `[LOCKED]`

In our era, **Taverns** are the empire-era equivalent of modern Pokémon Centers, but they serve *both* people *and* Pokémon. Each city has at least one Tavern, typically operated by a Templar Arceus presence.

**Functions:**
- **Heal Pokémon** to full (same as Pokémon Center function)
- **Issue a HealPass** after a full heal (carrying forward Dreamstone Mysteries' HealPass mechanic — see Section 11 Principle 4)
- **Lodge people** — players can pay (or earn through quest completion) overnight lodging, which advances day-to-night time progression
- **Social gathering space** — NPCs gather at Taverns. Many living-world dialogue beats happen here (Principle 1)
- **Source of Braviary travel** — the local Templar at each Tavern has a Braviary partner that the player can pay to ride between cities (Section 9)

**Design implication:** Taverns make cities feel *bigger and more lived-in* than modern Pokémon Centers do in DM. A Tavern is a *place to be* (drink, eat, gather information, sleep, meet NPCs) as well as a place to heal Pokémon. Each Tavern is a worldbuilding opportunity.

### The political crisis the cults have created `[LOCKED]`

The 9 Trial Houses are **divided in their loyalties**. Some remain loyal to the royal family. Others have been **infiltrated, corrupted, or actively recruited** by the Sword Cult (the dominant active cult; see Section 8). Silas Moray, as Vizier of Commerce and secret cult 2IC, has facilitated much of the corruption through psychic manipulation and intelligence-network co-option.

Part of the political intrigue of the game is **discovering which families are loyal and which have been turned**. The player must navigate this as they progress through the Trials. The corruption is widespread enough that **the empire's official intelligence apparatus is unreliable** — Nemo Korolev specifically built a *private mercenary intelligence network* that operates outside the corrupted official channels, because he could not trust the state apparatus to be uncompromised.

- `[OPEN]` Specific loyalty status of each Trial House at game start
- `[OPEN]` Geographic distribution of Trial Houses across Cormoria

### Cultural register `[WORKING]`

- Pre-industrial, mythologically dense
- Similar aesthetic register to PLA's Hisui but a *distinct civilization* — Cormorian visual identity
- `[OPEN]` Specific cultural-aesthetic inspiration

### Geography `[WORKING]`

The full Cormorian geographic inventory is documented in **Section 2 (Worldbuilding Foundations)** — it lists every confirmed Dreamstone Mysteries location and frames the geographic reuse principle (DM locations are temporally-aged versions of our empire-era versions).

Cormoria-specific notes for empire-era worldbuilding:
- **The Sevii Islands** were a Cormorian settlement, lost to Kanto when the Western Coalition war began. Their loss is a *casus belli* the player learns about.
- The **Cormorian capital** is `[OPEN]` — we have not yet chosen which DM location (or new location) is the imperial seat.
- **The Ashland Labyrinth** — House Ashland's subterranean fortress inside Mt. Ceram. Decays into Mt. Ceram Caves in DM's era. The deepest part contains a lava pool room which factors into the mid-game Mt. Ceram Beads-Cult-raid plot beat (Section 10).
- **The Mirroh Necropolis** — House Langerin's keep; the great mortuary structure of Cormoria. Decays into "Mirroh Base Camp Graveyard" in DM's era. **Contains a sealed gate to the Distortion World (Giratina's realm).** This is the canonical reason House Langerin specializes in Ghost-types and spiritual communion.
- **House Jalviss ruins** — the destroyed fortress of the destroyed Ground-type house. Location and accessibility TBD. The **Aerodactylite** is believed to still be there.
- **Cult stronghold geography** `[OPEN]` — Sword Cult and Beads Cult bases TBD; the Sword Cult, being dominant and political, probably has assets inside the capital and at various secret altars (one of which is the Hoya River altar where Baradus slaughtered the Tablets Cult in backstory).
- **The Paldean embassy or merchant compound** `[OPEN]` — given the Paldean funding pipeline for the cults, there is likely a Paldean diplomatic or commercial presence in Cormoria worth establishing.

---

## 5. Protagonist — Osrid / Rainhawk / Veddev

### Identity in three phases `[LOCKED]`

**The name architecture — three identities, three relationships with player-choice:**

1. **Osrid (true name)** — player can rename. This is what the player types at the start. The "real" name in the sense of the prince's birth-given identity. Used in private dialogue by inner-circle characters who know the truth.
2. **Nomad Alban / Rainhawk Alban / variants (overseas cover identity)** — **canonically locked.** Player cannot change "Nomad" or "Alban." Used by all generic NPCs throughout the entire game. The cover identity was constructed by Vizier Umbra to hide Osrid in plain sight during his 10-year overseas exile.
3. **Veddev (post-cataclysm final identity)** — **canonically locked regardless of player entry.** The name Vandras gives him after finding him post-cataclysm. The name Osrid *chooses* to keep going forward, despite remembering his original identity.

**This name architecture is itself an expression of the project's thesis: identity is something we receive in compassion, not something we are given by birth or by the state. The player's chosen name matters during the journey. The cover identity is a cage the system forces on him. The name given in love (Veddev) is the name he chooses to keep.**

**Phase 1: The Prince (pre-game backstory)**
- Canonical name: **Prince Osrid**, First Prince of Cormoria
- Younger brother of Queen-Regent Rhydia (Osrid is the elder twin; Rhydia is the younger twin)
- **The reason he was sent to war:** *Not* a standard military draft. **Vizier Umbra deliberately got him out of the country** after Eldred's assassination 10 years before the story. Umbra calculated that Osrid was the next assassination target as the last male heir and that protecting Rhydia and Pierra was easier with Osrid removed from the country entirely. **War service was protective cover, not a coming-of-age path.**
- **Umbra constructed a false identity for Osrid: "Nomad Alban"** — a fictitious bastard child of House Alban (Grass-type masters, see Section 2 and Section 4). House Alban was quietly complicit in the cover, acknowledging the prince as their tolerated bastard cousin during the entire 10-year overseas service. The choice was strategic: "Nomad" is the Cormorian generic name for unwanted noble children (Section 2 — Nomad cultural designation), and *nobody investigates a Nomad's history because nobody cares about Nomads.*
- **Umbra remained in continuous clandestine contact with Osrid for the entire 10 years.** Umbra is the *only person in Cormoria* who knew Osrid was alive during the false-death disinformation campaign.
- **Osrid's combat mentor was Boran Surge** (Windshear rank, minor noble of House Surge — see Section 7). Boran trained Osrid in combat and Pokemon command, but **Boran never knew his mentee was the prince.** He believed he was training "some Cormorian Nomad" — a tolerated bastard cousin of House Alban. The corruption Boran inflicted on Osrid's spirit (the "Pokemon are purely military assets" philosophy) was therefore *incidental*, not targeted — it was simply how Boran treats every soldier under his command.
- **The Nomad-Alban cover identity made Osrid more vulnerable to the corruption.** Performing marginality so thoroughly for 10 years — being told by social cues that he was tolerated rather than valued — left him without firm self-concept to push back against Boran's worldview. **The false identity was carefully designed to be invisible, and inadvertently became spiritually receptive.** This was not Umbra's intent.
- Earned the rank of **Rainhawk** for leading humans and Pokémon to victory against the Western Coalition. Promotion criteria: killed 3+ high-priority targets, demonstrated leadership across multiple campaigns. **The empire promoted "Rainhawk Alban" not knowing he was the missing prince** — they saw a Nomad who somehow outperformed the proper noble heirs, and grudgingly granted him the rank. Darkly ironic: the meritocratic system independently confirmed the royal birthright already established, *despite* the system's prejudices against Nomads.
- Was **severely scarred** during an operation in the Sevii Islands near the end of his overseas service
- After Sevii: awarded a special **Noh-styled ceremonial Rainhawk mask** with an **eye-of-Horus pattern** on the front, given to him to cover the scarring. **The mask is recent — it dates from the Sevii incident, not from the start of his exile.** Most of his 10-year exile was lived under a *named* false identity (Nomad Alban) rather than a masked one. Umbra arranged Osrid's return to Cormoria after the Sevii incident, behind the new mask.
- The Ruin Cults spread false news of his death overseas as an early strategy to destabilize Cormoria and isolate Rhydia. **Only Umbra knew the death-rumors were lies** — and Umbra deliberately did not correct them, judging that Osrid's safety required the deception to stand.
- The player can rename him; "Osrid" is the canonical name. **The player's chosen name applies only to the true-name layer.** The cover identity names (Nomad, Alban) and the final identity name (Veddev) are locked regardless of player entry.

**The mask's multi-layered symbolism `[LOCKED]`:**

The mask is *not* a 10-year companion, but its symbolic weight is dense. It carries simultaneously:

1. **Practical wound coverage** — the Sevii scarring caused real physical damage; the mask is medically/practically functional.

2. **Identity protection on return** — Umbra arranged Osrid's homecoming behind the mask deliberately. The cult assumed Osrid was dead; revealing his face on return would have signaled his survival.

3. **Emotional crutch and safety blanket** — *this is the deepest layer.* After 10 years of constructed-identity living (Nomad Alban — a real name and history but not his own), returning home to *his actual identity* would be terrifying. The mask lets him be *visibly* hidden in a way the Nomad-Alban identity wasn't — the mask is *honestly* a barrier rather than dishonestly a substitute. **Wearing it gives him permission to be Rainhawk rather than Osrid during a period when he is not yet ready to be the prince again.**

4. **Symbol of his entire decade** — even though the mask is recent, it *crystallizes* what 10 years of false-identity living did to him. The mask is the visible expression of a habit of hiding that started long before the mask itself. **He doesn't need the mask to hide; he needs the mask because hiding has become part of who he is.**

5. **Symbol of his self-concept** — Osrid has lived as someone other than himself for so long that his "real face" feels foreign to him. The mask is the face he recognizes. *Removing the mask is partly removing the face he's used to having.*

6. **The Eye of Horus iconography** — the symbol on the mask is associated with *protection, healing, and restoration* in ancient Egyptian mythology. A mask depicting the eye of restoration *over a wounded face* is the visible metaphor for Osrid's whole journey: he is wounded, he hides the wound, but the symbol on his hiding-place is *the symbol of healing*. **The mask was right; he was being restored. He just couldn't see his own face during the process.**

7. **The scarring beneath is mild** — by endgame the physical scarring has faded to where his face just looks "a little more lined than a normal person." **The mask is not held in place by his wounds. It is held in place by his fear of being seen.** The wounds were the *occasion* for the mask, but the *reason* it stays on is psychological. His fear of being seen was always larger than the thing he was afraid of revealing.

**Phase 2: The Rainhawk (the game)**
- **Wears the empire-era Rainhawk mask constantly throughout the main game** — including when alone, when with Rhydia, when with Umbra, when with Eden. **He never removes the mask privately.** The mask comes off exactly *once* during the entire game, in the Hall of Fame unmasking sequence (described below).
- Most NPCs address him with combinations of his cover identity: **"Nomad," "Rainhawk Alban," "the new Rainhawk," "Nomad Rainhawk"** (slightly contemptuous), or **"Rainhawk"** alone (respectful military shorthand)
- Inner-circle characters who know his true identity address him by his player-chosen true name (default "Osrid")
- **For the first half of the game, only Umbra knows Osrid is the prince.** Not Rhydia. Not Pierra. Not Glaive. Even Rhydia does not know her brother is alive until ~50% through the story, when she joins Osrid for a Sword Cult encounter and the truth is revealed under combat conditions.
- **Glaive learns** Osrid's identity later — after the Rhydia reveal, when Glaive's own arc demands it
- **Silas Moray** learns last — only at the moment of the public unmasking
- The rings of trust expand outward from Umbra → Rhydia → Glaive → the larger empire post-unmasking

**The Hall of Fame Unmasking Scene — climax of Act V `[LOCKED]`:**

The culmination of Osrid's character arc. The single moment when the mask comes off.

**Sequence:**
1. Osrid defeats Rhydia in the Champion battle
2. Both enter the **Hall of Fame** — the empire-era proto-Pokemon League Hall of Fame. **Osrid's team is preserved using daguerreotype-equivalent pre-industrial photographic technology** (or a pre-photographic mechanical-magical equivalent — see Section 9). The image captures the Champion and his team at the moment of victory; the image is then placed in a vault to eternally preserve the moment. **Osrid himself is captured in the photograph still masked.** The eternal record preserves Rainhawk Alban — *the masked Champion*. After Osrid removes the mask outside, the Hall photograph still shows him in it. **Centuries later, scholars and historians studying that image will see a masked Champion and may never know the man beneath. The mask becomes a museum artifact in the same moment Osrid sheds it.**
3. Osrid leaves the Hall of Fame with Rhydia and emerges to a **massive public crowd** waiting outside
4. **Osrid removes the mask in front of the crowd.** Public, maximally visible. The crowd sees a face they don't recognize — a slightly weathered young man with mild lining around his features (the faded scarring).
5. **Rhydia announces his true identity** — speaking the name "Osrid" (or the player-chosen true name) to Cormoria. **For most of the crowd, this is the moment they realize they have been celebrating the missing prince.**
6. Rhydia names him **Regent-King and Champion of Cormoria** — transferring both her political authority and her battle authority to him. *She becomes free of the regency she didn't fully want and the Championship she earned at the cost of believing her brother was dead.*

**Why this moment lands so powerfully:**
- Osrid does not remove the mask alone, or privately, or to a single trusted figure. **He removes it to a massive public.** This is the ultimate vulnerability — not "I am ready to be seen by one person" but "I am ready to be seen by everyone."
- The crowd has been celebrating *Rainhawk Alban's* victory. Then they see his face, hear his true name, and realize they have been celebrating the *missing prince*. **This becomes the defining moment of an entire generation of Cormorians** — *"I was there when the prince returned."*
- **Rhydia is seeing her brother's adult face for the first time in 10 years.** She knew him as a 10-year-old; she sees him as a 20-year-old in the moment of his coronation. *For Rhydia, the unmasking is also a reunion with the face she remembered as a child.* The political moment and the family moment collapse into one.
- **The mild scarring is the perfect ironic detail.** Osrid built up the mask as a barrier against *something terrible* — and when he finally removes it, the terrible thing he was hiding turns out to be *just a slightly more weathered face*. His fear was disproportionate to the reality. **The mask was always heavier than the face beneath.**
- **Parallel to Glaive's heart-opening evolution.** Glaive's Shelgon refused to evolve until he opened his heart; Osrid's face refused to be shown until he opened his identity. Both characters held back by their own self-protection; both transformations happen at the same kind of internal threshold.

**Phase 3: Veddev — the chosen identity (epilogue) `[LOCKED]`**

This is the project's most distinctive narrative move and one of its boldest departures from genre conventions.

- After the sealing cataclysm (Section 10), Osrid is presumed dead by Cormoria. The empire mourns its Regent-King and Champion. Pierra and Rhydia mourn him as lost. **Cormoria does not learn that he survived.**
- He is found post-cataclysm by **Vandras Amber-Letz** (merchant, eventually direct ancestor of Volo and distant ancestor of Cynthia). **Vandras has no idea who Osrid is.** Vandras adopts him as a younger brother in pure compassion-for-a-stranger.
- Vandras gives him the name **Veddev** — a name with no political weight, no royal blessing (Vandras has no surname-bestowing authority; Veddev is not an Arceus-blessed name). It is simply the name a stranger gives in love.
- **Osrid remembers everything.** His memory was not wiped by the sealing cataclysm. He retains every memory of his childhood, his exile, his mentorship under Boran, his return, the Trials, the unmasking, the Champion battle, the cult crisis, Silas's death, Nemo's death, the sealing.
- **Osrid chooses to discard his old identity completely.** He chooses to accept Veddev as his name going forward. He chooses not to return to Cormoria. He chooses not to inform Pierra or Rhydia that he survived. He chooses to leave the prince-life entirely behind.

**Why Osrid chooses Veddev:**
- He set out to save his family and right the wrongs done to his father. **He did that.** The revenge — in the sense of paying back what was owed — is complete.
- He does not want to continue being defined by the wrongs that were done. He wants to live in the future the saving made possible.
- He has no desire to be a prince or rule Cormoria. **The role is finished.** He has done what he set out to do; he gets to choose what comes next.
- **A brand new name, a new family (Vandras's), and Eden — these are all he needs to move forward.**
- He is leaving the past behind for good, but keeping what is ultimately most important to him for the future.

**This is renunciation as character culmination, not loss.** Most Pokemon player-protagonists end their journeys with their identity intact. Ours ends with **the protagonist remembering everything but choosing a new name to carry forward.** Identity is something we receive in compassion (Vandras's gift of the name Veddev) and *choose to keep* — not something we are given by birth or by the state. **Osrid's choice to keep "Veddev" over "Cormoria" is a quiet theological statement: he chooses the surname-of-love over the surname-of-divinity.**

**The journey through Paldea:**
- Veddev travels east with Vandras's entourage
- Delivers the four sealed cursed objects to the treasure-loving Paldean king (the canonical Paldean sealer per SV lore — Veddev's role in canonical history is preserved)
- **During the transaction, Eden walks in.** This is the moment that completes Veddev's transformation.

**The Eden reunion — pre-recognition and the completion of renunciation `[LOCKED]`:**
- Veddev is in Paldea performing the transaction as someone who has *committed* to leaving his past behind. He has decided not to be Osrid anymore. He has folded that life away.
- **Eden walks through the door — and his commitment to forgetting collapses at exactly the place where it could not hold.**
- It is not love-at-first-sight in the conventional sense. It is **conscious memory refusing to be renounced when faced with its actual referent.** Veddev sees her, and the renunciation breaks open. *The presence of Eden completes his renunciation by giving him something better to choose than the past.*
- **Eden, for her part, recognizes him in stages.** She had loved the masked Rainhawk for the latter half of the game without ever seeing his face. She saw his face briefly at the Hall of Fame unmasking. Now she sees him again, unmasked, in Paldea — older, more weathered, presenting himself as a merchant. **She recognizes his body language first** (pre-verbal, pre-rational — she has loved this body's bearing for years), **then his face**, then accepts that this is the same man.
- **Eden chooses to call him Veddev** going forward. She does not insist on the prince-name. She is meeting him in the present, accepting the identity he has chosen, recognizing that her love does not depend on which name he carries. **Her love adapts to his new identity rather than dragging him back to his old one.**
- He keeps remembering everything else of his prior life — but **only Eden matters enough to take with him.** Cormoria continues without him. His mother continues without him. His sister continues without him. The empire he saved continues without him. *He is not coming back.* **Eden is the only thing strong enough to make him keep something from his old life.**

**The marriage and after:**
- Veddev and Eden marry in Paldea
- They build a new life together
- They may have a hand in sealing the Treasures further (working alongside the Paldean king) and establishing the proto-detective tradition
- **Their children carry the Amber-Letz family name** — Vandras's family by adoption. The Amber-Letz name evolves to **Berlitz** across ~1,800 years (Cynthia's canonical family name)
- **Vandras never learns who Veddev "really is."** The greatest fan-project Pokemon family lineage we are establishing (Berlitz → Volo → Cynthia) descends from a man whose adoptive family never knew the truth of his origin. Vandras's compassion saved the Prince of Cormoria, and Vandras dies a stranger to that fact.
- **Cormoria mourns Osrid eternally.** Pierra outlives her son's *choice not to come home*, not just her son's apparent death in the sealing. Rhydia lives the rest of her reign believing she lost him twice — first to the false news 10 years earlier, then for real in the cataclysm. **Neither learns the truth.** This is *another sacrifice* Osrid makes — he doesn't get reunion with his family in exchange for being free of them.

### Character arc `[LOCKED]`

Osrid's arc has *multiple* layered emotional axes; this is one of the most psychologically detailed protagonists in fan-project Pokemon. Each axis develops in parallel across the game and culminates with renunciation:

**The "Pokémon as partners" axis (the project's central thesis):**
- **Begin:** Distrustful of grand causes; commanded Pokémon as a soldier but never truly bonded with one. **Has internalized Boran Surge's "Pokemon as weapons" philosophy** through nearly a decade of mentorship. This is not Osrid's natural disposition — he was a kind child before the war — but it is what his combat training inculcated.
- **Inciting moment:** Vizier Umbra gives him a *true partner Pokémon* — one of three unique starters (Frigibax, Teddiursa, Tinkatink), hand-selected for the prince's role. **Umbra's gift is the deliberate beginning of Osrid's re-learning of partnership** — bookending the Boran-Surge corruption with a renewed thesis.
- **Middle:** Investigates cult activity. Develops emotional bonds with his team that contrast with his military command-and-control mindset. Notable beats include the Crabominable adoption scene (Section 8 — Baradus profile, Section 11 — Principle 1+2 Exemplar), where the player can *consciously choose* compassion against Osrid's 10 years of Boran-Surge conditioning.
- **Late game:** Re-internalizes partnership through cumulative choices and the unmasking moment.
- **Climax:** Wields partnership at cosmic scale in the sealing ritual, with Ting-Lu (Nemo's gift) and his own party Pokemon.

**The identity-concealment trauma axis:**
- **Background:** Osrid has been hiding his identity for *10 years*, since he was 10 years old. **Hiding is his normal state.** Lived as Nomad Alban overseas. Returned home behind a mask he was given mid-Sevii operation.
- **Internal conflict throughout the game:** Should he reveal himself and reclaim his royal identity? Or should he complete the Trials *as Rainhawk* and quietly disappear, seeking a new life elsewhere? **The idea of unmasking himself and letting everyone know he's alive terrifies him** because he's been hidden for the entirety of his self-conscious life.
- **This is a core emotional beat of the whole story**, alongside the partnership thesis.
- **Unmasking is a real sacrifice**, not a triumph. He is not "claiming his rightful identity" but *giving up the safety of anonymity* that he has *needed* for a decade. The Hall of Fame unmasking happens because he *has to* (to save the empire, to confront the cults, to face Silas, to be Champion + Regent-King). Not because he wants to.

**The renunciation arc — the bravest narrative move:**
- Osrid sacrifices his hidden-Rainhawk identity to become public Prince/Champion/Regent-King. He does the unmasking he had been terrified of. He pays the cost of being known.
- He survives the sealing cataclysm (memory intact). **He chooses to leave the prince-identity behind anyway.** Not because he was forced to, but because he had already done what he set out to do.
- **Veddev in Paldea is what Osrid originally wanted to be:** someone with a peaceful life, no royal expectations, no need to be the prince. *He chooses this life consciously, with his memory intact.* **He is not running away. He is choosing forward.**
- The man who had every reason to be a prince and chose to be a merchant instead. **The man whose identity is not what he was given by birth but what he chose by love.**

**Eden as the only kept piece of the past:**
- Eden is the one thing Osrid is *unwilling* to leave behind. Everything else — Cormoria, his mother, his sister, the empire he saved, the royal birthright, the very name "Osrid" — he leaves. Eden he keeps.
- **She becomes the continuation of him**, not the keeper of his old self. Their marriage is between Veddev and Eden, not between Osrid-with-Eden.

### The double irony of Osrid's Rainhawk rank `[LOCKED]`

There is significant irony in the fact that Osrid rose through the military ranks on his own merits, but it turns out **he is the highest-ranking noble all along and never needed the prestige.** The empire promoted him to Rainhawk seeing a Nomad who somehow outperformed the proper noble heirs; he was, unbeknownst to them, the missing prince to whom the empire owed everything. **The meritocratic system independently confirmed the royal birthright already established**, *despite* the system's prejudices against Nomads — a quiet validation of Cormoria's deepest political principle (merit reflects truth) layered with darkly ironic dramatic structure (the system's confirmation was never needed).

And the final irony: **after all his identities have been confirmed — Nomad, Rainhawk, Prince, Champion, Regent-King — he discards them all in favor of Veddev, the name a merchant gave him in compassion.** *None of his earned or inherited names were what he chose to keep.*

**The twin-mirror dynamic with Rhydia `[LOCKED]`:**

Osrid and Rhydia are twins who responded to the same family trauma (their father Eldred's assassination at age 10) in *opposite* ways. **Both responses are coping mechanisms for the same wound, but they pull in opposite directions.**

- **Osrid went inward.** He was sent overseas under a false identity (Nomad Alban), then returned behind a Rainhawk mask. He has spent 10 years *hidden*. His instinct under pressure is to disappear, to conceal, to occupy a smaller and smaller visible self. The mask is the literal embodiment of this instinct.
- **Rhydia went outward.** She stayed in Cormoria, took over her mother's regency in all but formal title, became the Champion, and made herself the most publicly visible figure in the empire. She does the work of state on the front lines, mingling with the people, being seen constantly. Her popular legitimacy is *earned through visibility*, not granted by ceremony.

**The twins are mirror images of each other in their relationship to identity.** Osrid is the prince who refuses to be seen; Rhydia is the princess who makes herself constantly visible. Each is incomplete without the other.

When they reunite at ~50% (Rhydia learns Osrid is the new Rainhawk), **they begin in some sense learning from each other** about how to inhabit royal identity. Osrid may teach her something about the cost of visibility (he has paid that cost in shadow form for a decade); Rhydia may teach him something about the courage of being seen. By the Hall of Fame unmasking moment at the climax of Act V, **Osrid has integrated Rhydia's lesson** — he has become visible, claimed his identity publicly. By the renunciation epilogue, **he has *also* completed his own arc** — he chooses, having been visible, to disappear again, this time on his own terms rather than to protect himself.

Rhydia continues as Champion after Osrid disappears — but **she carries forward something of his integrated learning.** She has seen what it cost him to be visible; she may govern with more compassion for those who choose privacy as a result.

### Mechanics tied to the character `[OPEN]`

- Does the Rainhawk mask grant any mechanical effect? (likely no — the mask is symbolic, not mechanical)
- How is the Hall of Fame unmasking sequence implemented? (custom event with image-capture animation; sprite swap from masked to unmasked; daguerreotype-equivalent photo asset for the vault preservation)
- How is the Veddev epilogue implemented? (new sprite for Veddev distinct from both masked Rainhawk and unmasked Champion-Osrid; player-chosen true name no longer appears in dialogue after the renunciation)

## 6. The Starter Trio and Umbra's Gift Scene

### The gift scene `[LOCKED]`

After the prince returns home and reconnects with Rhydia, **Vizier Umbra** presents him with a choice between three Pokémon — hand-selected over years of search. Umbra's dialogue (paraphrased; refine later):

> *"These Pokémon are the rarest of the rare and uniquely colored in a way that has never been seen before in these species. You are our one and only Prince, and it is fitting that you begin your journey with a Pokémon partner that befits your role in the world. You will carry a heavy weight, and you need a unique power to rise above it. These three Pokémon I have painstakingly sought out possess that power."*

The player chooses one. The other two are not encountered in the wild — they are *uniquely-bred individuals* in Umbra's care.

### The Eden theft scene — Act I inciting incident `[LOCKED v0.9.7]`

Immediately after the gift scene, **Eden breaks into the basement of Umbra's estate in the starter town** (Silver/Crystal homage explicitly intended). She steals **the starter that is mechanically strongest against Osrid's pick**, leaving the third with Umbra.

**The type-counter steal logic `[LOCKED v0.9.7]`:**

| Osrid picks | Eden steals | Stays with Umbra | Counter logic |
|---|---|---|---|
| Frigibax (Dragon/Ice) | **Tinkatink** (Fairy/Steel) | Teddiursa | Fairy is 2× into Dragon; Steel resists Ice. Eden's stolen Tinkatink is *the dragon-slayer-bloodline pick weaponized against the dragon-line Osrid chose.* |
| Teddiursa (Normal → Normal/Ground) | **Frigibax** (Dragon/Ice) | Tinkatink | Ice STAB hits Ground super-effectively (Bloodmoon Ursaluna); Dragon-type raw offense is a special-attack threat against the Normal/Ground special-attacker Osrid raised. |
| Tinkatink (Fairy/Steel) | **Teddiursa** (Normal → Normal/Ground) | Frigibax | Ground hits Steel super-effectively (the late-game Bloodmoon Ursaluna's Ground STAB demolishes Tinkaton's Steel half). |

**The narrative function:**
- **Eden begins her bond-through-theft arc.** This is the inciting moment of *her* deuteragonist journey (Section 7 — Eden, expanded v0.9.7). She does not yet love the Pokemon she just stole. She will. Over the course of the game, **the stolen starter becomes the partner she chose by accident and kept by love.**
- **Eden keeps the stolen starter through the entire game — including post-game and her eventual marriage to Veddev in Paldea.** *Their post-game marriage is between two people who each still carry their original starter.* The mirror is the resolution: rivalry → true love, theft → bond, the partnership-thesis applied to *their own relationship* as well as to Pokemon.
- **The unstolen-third-starter stays with Umbra.** This Pokemon plays no role until the Act V Vizier gauntlet, where **Umbra deploys it as part of his team in the first Vizier fight** (see Section 10 — Act V; Section 7 — Cadmus Umbra). The third starter Osrid did not choose becomes one of the Pokemon Osrid must defeat to become Champion. **All three starters are present in the climactic political-progression arc:** the player's pick is in the player's party, Eden's pick is on Eden's team, Umbra's pick is on Umbra's team. **No starter is ever wasted; all three are part of the story's spine.**

**Eden's targeting was not random:**
- Eden has type-matchup knowledge appropriate to a *competent trainer*, not an amateur thief. She picked the *mechanically optimal* counter-starter, not a random target.
- This implies *someone* — likely Goma personally, or possibly a Beads Cult bishop — *directed* her to the basement with specific intelligence about Osrid's starter choice. **The theft is a targeted operation, not a freelance crime.** The Beads Cult's intelligence apparatus is documented in Section 8 (Goma's pre-Mt-Ceram operational capability).
- **Eden does not yet know she's being used.** She believes the theft is her own clever crime; she will eventually learn that her cult handlers leveraged her for this op. Her defection trajectory (Section 7 — Eden) accelerates partly because *she realizes she was a tool*.

**Umbra's response and the Mega-Dreamstone Shard briefing:**
- After Eden escapes, **Umbra briefs Osrid on a parallel main quest** (Section 9 — The Mega-Dreamstone Shard Quest): clandestinely recover the **9 scattered shards** of Cormoria's lost Mega-Dreamstone, intelligence on which comes from **Nemo Korolev's counter-intelligence network**. This briefing is the moment Osrid learns about the secret cosmological objective running parallel to the public Trial circuit.
- **The shard-quest briefing is conditional on the theft having just happened.** Umbra waits for the theft because (a) it confirms cult intelligence has noticed Osrid's existence, raising the stakes; (b) it gives Umbra moral cover to deputize the prince for clandestine operations the empire's official apparatus cannot be trusted with.

`[OPEN]` Specific dialogue beats and scene blocking of the theft + briefing sequence
`[OPEN]` Whether the player witnesses the theft directly or arrives just after Eden escapes
`[OPEN]` Whether the theft has a brief combat encounter (Eden vs. Osrid before she escapes with the stolen starter) or is purely cinematic
`[OPEN]` Eden's specific intel source (Goma directly? a Beads Cult bishop?)

### The three starters `[LOCKED — updated v0.9.7]`

| Species | Final form | Type | Role in trio | Thematic anti-Ruin coverage |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Frigibax | Baxcalibur (+ Mega Baxcalibur post-Champion) | Dragon/Ice | **Physical offense apex** — slow to develop, peaks highest, Mega Evolution unlocked via post-Champion Ancient Cormoria side quest | Mid against half the Treasures (Ice weak to Steel/Fire); priority covers Speed weakness via Mega |
| Teddiursa (Blue Moon) | Bloodmoon Ursaluna (Blue Moon) | Normal → Normal/Ground | **Special offense apex** — custom Sp.Atk-focused variant of Cormorian lineage; Ancient Peat Block + Lunacy unlocked via post-Champion Ancient Cormoria side quest | Strong vs. Chi-Yu (Ground STAB); strong vs. Ting-Lu (Moonblast covers); Ground-typed Blood Moon is the strongest Ground special move in the game |
| Tinkatink | Tinkaton (+ Divine Cuirass post-Champion) | Fairy/Steel | **Defensive specialist** with hammer offense and Pixilate-Hyper Voice sweeper option; Divine Cuirass unlocked via post-Champion Ancient Cormoria side quest | **Best anti-Ruin defensive coverage** (Fairy resists Dark; Steel coverage) |

**The starter trio's offensive/defensive trinity `[LOCKED]`:**

Each starter occupies a distinct mechanical role:

- **Frigibax → Baxcalibur** — *I want to hit hard physically and have priority + Mega options*
- **Teddiursa (Blue Moon) → Bloodmoon Ursaluna (Blue Moon)** — *I want to nuke things with special attacks and have Ground coverage with snowball potential*
- **Tinkatink → Tinkaton** — *I want to outlast everything and counter-punch with defensive reliability + Pixilate offense*

**No two are interchangeable. No pick is objectively weaker.** Each path has a clear Trial-6 Master Tutor evolution gate, a memorable ability suite creating multiple builds, a distinct combat identity, and a post-Champion Ancient Cormoria side quest that unlocks the line's coronation-tier bonus item. **This is the design level where players love their starter choice for personal mechanical reasons, not just narrative ones.**

### Mechanical implementation `[LOCKED]`

- **All three are true shinies with new custom palettes** (not the canonical shiny colors)
- Requires designing 9 new sprite palettes (3 evolution lines × 3 stages each), plus their overworld variants
- Shiny status is flagged genuinely in the data — they are mechanically shinies, not cosmetic-only
- **Each starter line has its OWN unique shiny palette identity** — the three starters do NOT share a visual scheme. Each gets a one-of-a-kind look so that a player's choice of starter results in a genuinely distinctive partner.
- Within an evolution line (e.g., Frigibax → Arctibax → Baxcalibur), the palette identity stays consistent so the line reads as a cohesive family.
- **Tinkaton's BST is buffed to BST 560 `[LOCKED v0.9.7]`** — final stat line **75 / 85 / 125 / 75 / 125 / 75** (HP / Atk / Def / SpA / SpD / Spe). Parity with Bloodmoon Ursaluna (BST 560); Baxcalibur sits at canonical BST 600. The three starters' base-apex forms span BST 560-600, then *coronation-tier bonus forms* (Mega Baxcalibur 720, Bloodmoon Ursaluna with Lunacy, Tinkaton with Divine Cuirass) form the parallel competitive tier. **Tinkatink and Tinkatuff stat buffs `[OPEN]`** — to be locked alongside the line's full custom learnset

**Teddiursa line `[LOCKED v0.9.6 — Bloodsnow visual identity, v10 palette shipped]`:**

Cormorian "Blue Moon" / "Bloodsnow" lunar-mystic identity — **snow-white fur as the dominant silhouette color** with **blood-red lunar markings** (forehead crescent on Teddiursa, chest ring on Ursaring, forehead moon + eye on Bloodmoon Ursaluna) and **rich chocolate-brown anatomical details** on Bloodmoon Ursaluna (arms, spike-tips, armor plates). The snow-and-blood palette reads as *moonlit ritual* — distinguishing the Blue Moon line from the canonical brown-warmth-bear identity while staying within a believable bear color space.

Design pivot history: v0.9.2 specified a deep-blue-and-black "moonlit night" palette per reference image. During v10 sprite production iteration (this session), the palette evolved through 10+ versions before locking on the Bloodsnow identity. The shift was driven by: (a) need for clear visual distinction from the Frigibax line (which uses cool-blue + gold heraldic), (b) the user's "bear with bloody moon symbol" intuition aligning with the Phancero / Cormor blood-pact lore, and (c) snow-white providing better contrast with red markings than navy at the GBA's 5-bit-per-channel color resolution.

**Tinkatink line `[LOCKED v0.9.7 — Dragon-Slayer Bloodline visual identity, palette work pending Phase 1 production session]`:**

Cormorian dragon-slayer-bloodline identity — the starter Tinkatink belongs to a *uniquely-colored* clan of Tinkatinks descended from an ancient Tinkaton Matriarch who sleeps in the deep desert (see "The Tinkatink Line — Cormorian Dragon-Slayer Bloodline" subsection below). The bloodline coloration is *what distinguishes this individual Tinkatink from canonical wild Tinkatinks* — it is the visible marker that this Pokemon descends from the clan that has been ecologically suppressing Cormoria's dragon population for centuries. Specific palette values TBD pending the Phase 1 sprite production session.

**Design constraint:** The line's palette identity must be *visually distinct from both the Frigibax line (Cormorian heraldic: charcoal + imperial gold + royal blue) and the Teddiursa line (Bloodsnow: snow-white + chocolate brown + blood red).* No shared color scheme.

**Mechanical note:** Like the Teddiursa line, the Tinkatink line is mechanically always-shiny from selection. **Palette changes go in `normal.pal`, not `shiny.pal`** — what the player sees *is* the normal form to them mechanically (see DREAMSTONE_HANDOFF.md Section 10.5).

### The Tinkatink Line — Cormorian Dragon-Slayer Bloodline `[LOCKED v0.9.7]`

The Tinkatink line in Dreamstone Ruination is *not* a wild-caught Tinkatink. It is a **uniquely-bloodlined individual** from an ancient dragon-slayer clan whose existence is the in-world explanation for Cormoria's chronic dragon scarcity.

**The Tinkaton Matriarch — the sleeping ancient `[LOCKED v0.9.7]`:**

Deep in the **far desert and surrounding mountains** of Cormoria lies an ancient cave system. Within it sleeps an **ancient Tinkaton Matriarch** — a Pokemon so old that her active leadership of her clan has long since passed into deep slumber. She is still alive; she simply *rests*, having earned her sleep after centuries of dragon-hunting.

**Her clan still lives in the desert and mountains around her cave.** Her uniquely-colored descendants — the dragon-slayer bloodline — defend the territory she carved out across centuries. **Their primary prey are Garchomp, Flygon, and the occasional Sandaconda** — dragons and dragon-egg-group Pokemon that wander the desert. The clan has been at this work *for so long* that they have effectively driven Cormoria's wild dragon population to near-extinction.

**This is the in-world explanation for several locked details across the bible:**
- **Why dragons are vanishingly rare in Cormoria** (Section 7 — Glaive). The empire credits Glaive as the first true dragon-master because *most Cormorians who might have pursued Dragon mastery had no dragons to find*.
- **Why the Solomonar title has been given only twice in 2000 years** (Section 2 — Solomonar title). The ecological suppression of dragons is centuries-old; opportunities for mastery are correspondingly rare.
- **Why the Frigibax line is so striking as a starter pick.** Umbra is presenting Osrid with a *dragon* in a region where wild dragons have been hunted to near-vanishing for centuries. *That* is why Frigibax was hand-selected as one of three starters — it represents the dragon-lineage that the empire has nearly lost.
- **Why Glaive is wary of the Tinkaton clan** (Section 7 — Glaive, updated v0.9.7). His Salamence is the *exact* kind of Pokemon the clan considers prey; his Sandaconda is *literally* listed as one of their primary kills. His unease is concrete, not abstract.

**The Matriarch as a parallel to Cormor `[LOCKED v0.9.7]`:**

The Tinkaton Matriarch occupies a *Pokemon-cosmological-tier longevity* slot that mirrors but does not duplicate Cormor's Avalon sleep (Section 9 — Cormor's True Status):
- **Cormor sleeps** in a distant-past Dreamstone-displaced location, awakening conditional on Keerin's judgment of cosmic need
- **The Matriarch sleeps** in an ancient cave in the deep desert, awakening conditional on a sufficient threat to her clan's territory

**Both are ancient beings retreated into deep slumber. Both are still alive. Both can wake.** The Matriarch is *not* pact-blessed (she falls outside the Cormor-Lethys-Roma-Cogita Pact-Extended Longevity framework of Section 9); her longevity is *Pokemon-cosmological-tier ancient* — a different mechanism entirely.

**The mid-game Tinkatink clan side quest serves as the project's small-scale rehearsal of the larger Cormor-might-wake possibility.** When the player intrudes deep enough into the cave during the mid-game quest, the Matriarch *does* wake — proving that ancient sleepers can be roused by sufficient cause. *This is the only such awakening the player witnesses in the game.* Cormor's potential awakening remains permanently open per design (Section 9). The Matriarch's awakening is the *limited* version of what Cormor's would be — a Pokemon-tier echo of the cosmic-tier mystery.

**The Tinkatink starter's specific origin `[LOCKED v0.9.7]`:**

Umbra visited the desert region during his years of searching for the three perfect starter partners. While in the clan's territory, **he found an egg** of the unique bloodline coloration. **The strong implication is that he took the egg without the clan's permission** — "stolen under their noses." He returned to his workshop with it and hatched it. The hatchling Tinkatink became the third of his three hand-selected starters.

**The starter Tinkatink does not know its heritage.** It was hatched in Umbra's workshop, raised by Umbra and his staff, and has no memory of the desert or the clan. To the Tinkatink, Umbra is its first family; Osrid (when chosen) becomes its second.

**Umbra knows the heritage.** And **Umbra briefly tells Osrid the truth** during a return visit to the workshop after the starter selection — *conditional on Osrid having chosen Tinkatink*. The scene is brief, almost offhand: Umbra acknowledges that the Tinkatink came from the desert clan, that he took the egg, and that the clan is one of the oldest Pokemon families in Cormoria. He does *not* dwell on the moral weight of the theft; **Umbra is, by his own private moral calculus, willing to commit lesser wrongs in service of preserving the empire's royal succession** (this is consistent with his bible-locked role of constructing Osrid's Nomad-Alban false identity, withholding Osrid's return from Rhydia for months, etc.). The egg-theft is, for Umbra, one of many quietly-carried compromises.

**Glaive's response to a Tinkatink-pick player `[LOCKED v0.9.7]`:**

Glaive's pre-battle and post-battle dialogue varies slightly depending on whether Osrid chose Tinkatink:
- **If Osrid chose Frigibax:** Glaive's locked v0.9.6 dialogue applies — extra respectful acknowledgment of the Frigibax as a fellow dragon (psychic-empathic recognition).
- **If Osrid chose Tinkatink `[LOCKED v0.9.7]`:** Glaive is markedly *wary*. His dialogue acknowledges the Tinkatink's bloodline — not with hostility, but with the unease of a dragon-master facing a creature whose ancestors have been hunting his Pokemon's kin to near-extinction. **Specific dialogue lines TBD.** Glaive does not hold the Tinkatink personally responsible (the Pokemon is a young hatchling, raised by Umbra, knows nothing of its heritage), but his body language and word-choice signal that he *understands* what is in the prince's party.
- **If Osrid chose Teddiursa:** Glaive's dialogue is the neutral baseline — no Frigibax-recognition, no Tinkatink-wariness.

**The mid-game Tinkatink clan side quest `[LOCKED v0.9.7 — structural lock; details TBD]`:**

Around the mid-game, the player can travel to the desert region and encounter the Tinkatink clan in their territory. **The quest has *more thematic weight* if the player is traveling with an Arctibax or Tinkatuff:**
- **Arctibax-bearing player:** Osrid arrives with the *dragon line* the clan has been hunting. The encounter is tense; the clan's clear ecological hostility toward dragons creates real combat stakes.
- **Tinkatuff-bearing player:** Osrid arrives with the *clan's own stolen child*, now mid-evolution. The encounter is one of recognition — the player's Tinkatuff is *of their bloodline*, raised away from them by the empire. Combat tension is overlaid with a kind of fraught reunion.
- **Bloodmoon-Ursaring-bearing player:** Neutral encounter (the clan has no specific ecological grudge against bears).

**Combat structure:** The player fights multiple **Tinkatinks** of the clan, then a few **Tinkatuffs**, then eventually **the Matriarch awakens** and the player faces her. **The Matriarch is guarding a Dreamstone fragment** — one of the 9 shards of the lost Mega-Dreamstone (Section 9 — Mega-Dreamstone Shard Quest). Defeating her is the *only* way to recover this shard.

**The Matriarch's combat profile `[OPEN]`:** Level cap, exact moveset, abilities all TBD. Given her status as an ancient apex-Tinkaton, she should significantly exceed Trial-cap encounters — possibly an above-cap encounter (precedent established by the Frigibax-picker post-Champion quest's level-120 Roaring Moon and Walking Wake; the Matriarch may be in a similar above-cap tier even at the mid-game placement).

**Post-quest implications:**
- The clan continues to defend its territory; the Matriarch returns to her cave to sleep again
- Osrid is one of the only outsiders to have entered the clan's domain and survived
- **The Tinkaton clan as a Cormorian institutional curiosity** is now documented in the player's experience — the engaged player understands why Cormorian dragons are rare in a way most NPCs never will

`[OPEN]` Specific naming for the Matriarch, the clan, the desert/mountain region
`[OPEN]` Whether the player can ever return to the Matriarch's cave (likely no — it's a single-window event)
`[OPEN]` Whether the Tinkatink-picker player has any *additional* dialogue with the Matriarch acknowledging the stolen-egg heritage
`[OPEN]` Whether the Baxcaliburite post-Champion quest ("The Origins of Cormoria's Dragons") references the Tinkaton clan's ecological role in dragon scarcity, or whether that quest stays focused on pre-Tinkaton ancient dragon lineage (Roaring Moon, Walking Wake)
`[OPEN]` Whether other named members of the clan appear (a Tinkaton lieutenant who defends the Matriarch's cave entrance, etc.)

### The Blue Moon Teddiursa Line — Cormorian Lunar-Mystic Variant `[LOCKED v0.9.2]`

The Teddiursa line in Dreamstone Ruination is *not* the canonical Teddiursa → Ursaring → Ursaluna evolutionary path. It is a **uniquely Cormorian "Blue Moon" variant** with the following distinguishing features:

**Visual identity:** Snow-white fur with blood-red lunar markings and (on Bloodmoon Ursaluna) chocolate-brown anatomical details (per v10 Bloodsnow palette locked v0.9.6 — see Section 5 visual identity subsection). The recolor reframes the line from "wild brawling bear" to "lunar ritual bear" — fitting the line's reimagined role as the trio's *Special Attack apex* and tying visually to the Phancero / Cormor blood-pact cosmology.

**Type:** Normal (Teddiursa, Ursaring) → Normal/Ground (Bloodmoon Ursaluna on evolution). The Ground secondary typing emerges with the final form, paralleling canonical Bloodmoon Ursaluna's typing.

**Evolutionary path:**
- Teddiursa (Blue Moon) → Ursaring (Blue Moon) at level 24 (standard level evolution)
- Ursaring (Blue Moon) → **Bloodmoon Ursaluna (Blue Moon)** at level 45 *after learning Blood Moon (Ground) from the Master Tutor* — **skipping the regular Ursaluna stage entirely**

The regular (brown) Ursaluna stage is not part of this variant's evolution. Players who choose Teddiursa receive the Cormorian Blue Moon variant from the start; their final form is Bloodmoon Ursaluna directly. This is consistent with Bloodmoon Ursaluna being canonically a form variant (not a separate evolutionary stage in the strict sense) and Ruination establishing the Cormorian Blue Moon lineage as a *unique regional variant* with its own evolutionary mechanics.

**Special Attack identity (stat redistribution) `[LOCKED v0.9.5]`:**

The Blue Moon line is the trio's special-attack apex. The line begins as a pure Atk↔SpA swap at the baby form, then progressively *redistributes toward bulk* in the mid-stage, then *gains a final-form BST increase* with further bulk shoring at evolution. The result is a *bulky-tank-mage archetype* that grows progressively more durable as it evolves.

| Form | Canonical stats (HP/Atk/Def/SpA/SpD/Spe) | Blue Moon stats | BST | Change from canonical |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Teddiursa (Blue Moon) | 60/80/50/50/50/40 | **60/50/50/80/50/40** | 330 | Pure Atk↔SpA swap; BST preserved |
| Ursaring (Blue Moon) | 90/130/75/75/75/55 | **100/75/85/100/85/55** | 500 | Swap + redistribute 30 from SpA into HP/Def/SpD (+10 each); BST preserved |
| Bloodmoon Ursaluna (Blue Moon) | 113/135/70/75/75/52 | **113/75/85/145/90/52** | 560 | SpA 75→145 (+70 apex), Def 70→85 (+15), SpD 75→90 (+15); BST increased from 520 to 560 |

**Design intent for the stat progression `[LOCKED v0.9.5]`:**

- **Teddiursa (Blue Moon):** Frail special attacker at baby form — pure swap, no rebalancing yet. Identity: *fragile lunar pup*.
- **Ursaring (Blue Moon):** The mid-stage *redistributes 30 points from SpA into bulk* — HP +10, Def +10, SpD +10, SpA −30. SpA drops from 130 to 100, which is still solid for a mid-evolution. The result: a *meaningfully bulkier* mid-form (100 HP / 85 Def / 85 SpD) that still hits hard with 100 SpA. Identity: *bulky special attacker still in training*.
- **Bloodmoon Ursaluna (Blue Moon):** The final form is the apex — SpA jumps to **145** (the special-offense ceiling of the entire starter trio), Def and SpD each get +15 buffs over canonical, and BST increases from 520 to 560. Identity: *apex special-attack tank — slow, bulky, devastating*.

**Why the rebalance was needed (v0.9.5 reasoning):**

The original v0.9.2 spec had a pure Atk↔SpA swap throughout the line. While elegant, this produced a final form with 135 SpA and 75/70/75 defenses — a *glass-cannon mage* archetype. **The v0.9.5 redistribution shifts the line's identity from "glass cannon" to "bulky tank-mage"** — a more thematically appropriate match for the Teddiursa/Ursaring/Ursaluna species' canonical heavy-bear-tank visual identity, and a more interesting late-game play experience (the starter survives long enough to make multiple offensive plays per battle, rather than being one-shot).

The +40 BST increase at the Bloodmoon Ursaluna stage gives the line a *clear power spike at final evolution* matching the post-Champion narrative significance of the form — Bloodmoon Ursaluna is one of only two Pokémon in the project rated at BST 560+ (the other being post-Mega Baxcalibur at canonical 720). This positions the Blue Moon line as competitive against the Frigibax line's apex form on the Sp.Atk axis.

`[OPEN]` Whether Bloodmoon Ursaluna's BST 560 needs re-balancing against the other two starters' apex forms (Mega Baxcalibur is canonically 720; Tinkaton's coronation-tier Divine Cuirass-boosted form is still being specified; comparison TBD)

**The four-ability suite `[LOCKED v0.9.2]`:**

The Blue Moon line has **two normal abilities + one Hidden ability + one post-Champion ability-replacement state**:

1. **Mind's Eye (normal)** — accuracy/evasion ignore + the user can hit Ghost-types with Normal and Fighting moves. *Offensive utility against the typical Normal-STAB wall.* This is canonically Bloodmoon Ursaluna's signature ability in the broader Pokemon universe; in Ruination it is carried down the *entire* Blue Moon line, available from Teddiursa onward. **Even at low levels, Teddiursa can use Echoed Voice / Round to hit Ghost-types.** This is a meaningful early-game advantage that makes the Blue Moon pick *immediately* competitive against Ghost-typing.

2. **Fur Coat (normal)** — halves damage from physical attacks. *Defensive utility against the line's biggest matchup weakness (physical attackers).* The line is a high-HP slow special-attacker; physical attackers that can outspeed and burst it down are its natural counter. Fur Coat addresses this directly — physical attackers now do half damage. Bloodmoon Ursaluna with Fur Coat is one of the most durable sweepers possible in the project. Also *thematically perfect*: a bear has a thick fur coat. Species-appropriate in a way the other transplanted abilities aren't.

3. **Soul-Heart (Hidden)** — +1 Sp.Atk stage each time another Pokemon faints. *Snowball offensive ability for endgame sweeps.* Every KO Bloodmoon Ursaluna scores increases its Sp.Atk by one stage. By the time it has KO'd 2-3 Pokemon, it's at +2 or +3 Sp.Atk and is hitting *exceptionally* hard with Blood Moon and Hyper Voice.

4. **Lunacy (post-Champion ability replacement via Ancient Peat Block)** — *new custom ability designed for Ruination.* Special-attack analog of Supreme Overlord (canonical Kingambit ability). Two-axis snowball:
   - **Passive at switch-in:** Speed receives a multiplicative boost based on number of fainted allies on the user's team at the moment of switch-in. Suggested implementation: 10% Speed per fainted ally, max 50% at 5 fainted allies. (Mirrors Supreme Overlord's "boost from allies' deaths" mechanic, but applied to Speed instead of Atk/SpA.)
   - **Reactive on KO:** Sp.Atk receives +1 stat stage each time the user KOs an opponent. (Mirrors Soul-Heart's mechanic but only triggers on the user's KOs.)
   - **Acquisition:** post-Champion side quest in Ancient Cormoria (see Special items and bonus content subsection below). The Ancient Peat Block, when consumed, *replaces* Bloodmoon Ursaluna's current ability with Lunacy. **This is a permanent transformation** — once Lunacy is gained, the prior ability is lost. The choice to consume the Peat Block is *one-way*.

**The three build paths (pre-Champion):**

- *Anti-Ghost mystic sweeper* with **Mind's Eye** — best in trial-and-coverage situations where Ghost-types would otherwise wall Normal STAB
- *Physical wall + special nuker* with **Fur Coat** — the most all-around durable build, devastating against physical-attacker-heavy opponent teams
- *Snowball lategame ace* with **Soul-Heart (HA)** — the cult-war / Champion-fight build, peaking when allies have fallen

**The endgame transformation (post-Champion):**

After becoming Champion and acquiring the Ancient Peat Block, the player can choose to consume it and unlock **Lunacy**. This is the line's *coronation-tier bonus*, parallel to Mega Baxcalibur (Frigibax) and Divine Cuirass (Tinkatink).

**Learnset architecture `[LOCKED]`:**

The Blue Moon line's learnset emphasizes Normal special STAB, Ground special coverage, and late-game utility:

**Teddiursa (Blue Moon):**
- Lv 1: Covet, **Echoed Voice** (special Normal STAB)
- Lv 5: **Round** (special Normal STAB)
- Lv 9: Fake Tears (drops opponent Sp.Def — synergistic)
- Lv 13: **Disarming Voice** (Fairy coverage)
- Lv 17: **Mud-Slap** (Ground special, introduces typing)
- Lv 21: **Mud Shot** (Ground special, 55 BP)
- **Lv 24: EVOLVES INTO URSARING (Blue Moon)**

**Ursaring (Blue Moon) — on-evolution + level-up additions:**
- Lv 24 (on evolution): **Hyper Voice** (90 BP Normal special STAB — major power spike)
- Lv 28: Mud Sport or Sandstorm (utility)
- Lv 32: **Mud Bomb** (Ground special, 65 BP)
- Lv 38: **Earth Power** (Ground special, 90 BP — first major Ground STAB)
- Lv 42: **Boomburst** (140 BP Normal special — climactic Ursaring move pre-Bloodmoon evolution)
- **Lv 45: EVOLUTION GATE** — visit the Blood Moon Master Tutor, learn Blood Moon (Ground), evolves to Bloodmoon Ursaluna (Blue Moon)

**Bloodmoon Ursaluna (Blue Moon) — on-evolution + level-up additions:**
- Lv 45 (on evolution via tutor): **Blood Moon (Ground)** — 140 BP, special, Ground type, cannot be used twice in a row consecutively (canonical Blood Moon restriction retained; only the type changes from Normal to Ground)
- Lv 50: **Hyper Beam** (Normal, 150 BP special with recharge — line's hard-hitting Normal special)
- Lv 55: **Moonblast** (Fairy, 95 BP special — coverage for Dark/Dragon, thematically resonant with the Blue Moon identity)
- Lv 60: **Calm Mind** (Sp.Atk + Sp.Def boost — setup option that pairs powerfully with Soul-Heart/Lunacy snowballing)
- Lv 65: **Slack Off** (recovery — endgame sustain)
